They know that the criticism of well-known constitutional experts is that the Austrian Constitution is overshadowed by individual regulations and therefore some constitutional articles are no longer anchored in the consciousness of the population. For the rights of young people and children, it would be too little for me that they are only anchored in the Constitution, while they are not anchored in the consciousness of the population. Therefore, I believe that the chosen path is the more sensible one.

ParlaMint-AT_2004-11-18-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00087_d7e2922	Dear Mrs Colleague! If you look at the 1995 procedure, you can see that the politicians who used to be with 55. The age of retirement was now equal to all other insured persons, so that the age of retirement gradually works for each year. It used to be very short insurance periods. The young politicians, who were not yet in Parliament for ten years on the date of the 1997 deadline, have a pension fund and no political pension. Only those who have been in politics for ten years and longer at the time still have the old right. In this old law, the regulations have been re-trained in civil service law, and there has been the same deterioration as for civil servants in the same form. Just as the transitional periods for retirement work, they work parallel and synergies for politicians. The past belongs to the fact that, in young people, half-time work as a minister could be retired with full retirement in the context of a derogation. The level of reference has also been significantly reduced down, and multiple pensions that were previously possible are no longer possible for young politicians or for politicians covered by the new policy rules. The pension scheme is very many pension schemes in the private sector and in other areas. I therefore believe that we can actually talk about a gradual harmonisation of policy systems with the rules for all other citizens in that country. What remains remains is that group of politicians who have fallen under the rules of the old law, among whom I also belong, which I have never kept silent in the public. For this area, however, we have gradually made a number of reductions, and those who are already in retirement must accept a pension contribution of 15 per cent if they are above the maximum basis of assessment of the ASVG with their pension, and of 8 per cent if they are below the ASVG limit with their pension. These are pension contributions which are significantly higher than those for the other citizens, but which are 15 percent within the jurisdiction of the Constitutional Court. We therefore believe that it will be pointless if old politicians turn to the Constitutional Court because they will not get the right there, but the cuts will apply to them for life.

The throughput, which prevails, is no longer to be reversed. So there's really a break that's going to be made there. This is a problem which, in my opinion, is constantly swinging in your reforms. A set-aside scheme, as proposed, would also have meant: Those who are shortly before the pension have very small supplements, have a very small share of the new system. The further you are away from the retirement age, the greater the share that is in the new system, and the lower the proportion that would have been borne in the old system. There would have been a smooth transition – which we do not now – and that is something we really think is problematic and not meaningful. The last point I would like to address is: Yesterday it was still said that one cannot be forced to change from unemployment to retirement; now this applies only limited, apparently limited to one year – pension corridor. The 62-year-olds cannot therefore be forced to retire immediately; they can then go into unemployment for one year. But at 63, if they don't find a job anymore, they have to retire. Now I am looking at the proportion of the 62-year-old unemployed who will be able to find a new job from 62 to 63! It's probably going to be relatively small. – Let’s just sum up what that means: One goes from 62 to 63 in unemployment, loses one year of income, loses one's earnings; this year you pay less pension insurance contributions, this makes you feel negative, because the pension is getting lower; and then you have a reduction of 4.2 per cent for the two years that you are forced to retire earlier, i.e. 8.4 per cent, which is added again. – This fact alone probably accounts for about 10 percent of the pension loss resulting from this measure – in addition to all the measures already taken with the last pension reforms. If someone else goes here and says that this is fair and fair, this is a relatively courageous announcement, but it has very little to do with reality.

Scheibner: Yes!]] It is clear from this that the family allowance for recognised refugees in Austria, whose status is almost equivalent to that of the Austrians and who receive the corresponding social benefits, is subject to a very serious restriction. – It has nothing to do with retirement, harmonisation and care! Rather, this is the retention of a performance that has been a very important contribution to the future integration of these people in Austria in the past – and this has been true for many years. I'll just explain it to you: People who have received asylum in Austria have also been granted family aid for children, from the time they have fled, as the definition of refugees is according to the Geneva Convention on Refugees. The Geneva Refugee Convention precisely defines that one is a refugee if one is for these or those reasons – and these are precisely defined! – is persecuted, and if you come to another state, you can get protection from persecution, which is ensured by appropriate procedures. Ladies and gentlemen! These procedures have traditionally lasted quite a long time in Austria. Some get asylum very quickly. There are such cases also in Austria, even now. But there are cases of refugees who receive asylum in Austria, who are now granted this status – I am not saying: this right, but: wait for this status – for a very long time and are ultimately granted this status as a recognised refugee under the Austrian Asylum Act after a process that has sometimes been extended for many years. These are people who are allowed to stay in this country because they are seeking protection from persecution, and according to the old legal situation – and this is still the case in the family law – it is the fact that someone is a refugee after the Geneva Convention on Refugees. Now that's changing. Now one is relying on the Austrian Asylum Act and says that only from the time when this Republic is able to grant this status to someone – sometimes after a long-term procedure – is due to this achievement. Ladies and gentlemen! Is this person – man, woman or child – responsible for the fact that for years the country in which he finds protection from persecution is unable to conclude a quite legitimate procedure? He can only get this performance at that time. And this abuse that lasts for years is something new, I tell you. He is therefore something new, because this Republic is unable to include those institutions – keyword: Bundesasylamt, keyword: Bundesasylsenat – to provide with human resources to guarantee this right to a fair trial within the meaning of a procedure to be concluded within a foreseeable period of time. You all certainly know refugee families. I know an Afghan refugee family, which was not granted asylum status after a completely sluggish asylum procedure in the first instance. This is not surprising in the working method of the Federal Asyl Office, where 60 percent of asylum applications are withdrawn and then landed at the UBAS. This refugee family, if everything goes well, may have a chance next year, mid-2005, to reach a hopefully positive outcome of the procedure at the UBAS. This is more than likely to be the outcome of the proceedings, according to the Judicature currently in force. Until then, this family will have been in Austria for a total of three and a half years and will of course stay here because these people can and will not go back. These people who are still asylum seekers under the Austrian Asylum Act, under the Geneva Convention on Refugees, now want to avoid this essential integration, which has been achieved in the past, because they have been given retroactive support in the form of the repayment of family aid. From this support, people can, when they are released from federal care and get basic care and then perhaps a home, for example a children's room facility and a desk and a computer for the children – because it's always about children, not about adults. If there is anyone here in the room on the right who can tell me what this has to do with pension insurance and pension harmonisation, then he still has about 7 minutes to do it!
